People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done what I can,” but my mind just keeps circling back anyway.

It’s silly, because I’ve always been a list person—my husband used to tease me about having lists for my lists—but lately it doesn’t give me the relief it used to. I’ll have everything written down and still sit there thinking, “Did I miss something?” and then I’m off again.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe for a moment, yes… or I think it’s more that doing the list or checking the date makes me feel like I’ve done the sensible thing. My father used to say, “Measure twice, cut once,” though he was talking about shelves, not appointments.

But the trouble is, even after I’ve made the list and checked what I needed to check, I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it starts. I’ll tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can,” and still my mind keeps going back to it. So it doesn’t really make me feel better for long.

So it doesn’t really make me feel better for long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors in a precise way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a ritual, exactly. I mean, I do like things done a certain way sometimes, but I think that’s just how I’ve always been. My kitchen cupboards, for instance—well, actually my daughter says I put the mugs in the “wrong” place, so perhaps it’s not even that organized.\n\nBut no, I don’t have a particular thing I feel I must do just so, or something bad will happen if I don’t. At least, I haven’t noticed anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, more just wanting to feel like I’ve kept on top of things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them or think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually the main thing that brought me in. It’s not usually one single thought like a phrase repeating, more like the worrying gets going and I can’t seem to switch it off.\n\nI’ll have made the list, checked the dates, done what I can—my daughter teases me about my little scraps of paper everywhere, though I think she forgets she does the same thing on her phone—and even then my mind keeps circling back. I’ll be trying to read or make dinner, and it’s still there, nagging at me, even when I tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can.”\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have any intrusive thoughts that come into your mind unwanted and uninvited?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I suppose they are unwanted in the sense that I’m not inviting them in. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Right, let’s worry for the next hour.” It just starts up.

I’ll have done the sensible things — made my list, checked the dates, made sure I haven’t forgotten something — and then I’ll be washing a cup or looking for my glasses, which are usually on my head, silly as that is, and the worry is right back there again. I tell myself I’ve done all I reasonably can, but it doesn’t seem to switch off just because I say so.

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… no. I mean, I do think things through over and over, but not in the sense that I have to picture something or say something in my head to keep everyone safe, if that’s what you mean.

My sister used to say a little prayer before long drives—well, not my sister, actually, my aunt did that—and I suppose lots of people have little habits like that. But for me, it’s more that my mind wanders back to the worry. I haven’t noticed having a particular thought or image that I feel I must have, or else something bad will happen.

It’s more that I sometimes want to be certain I haven’t forgotten something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying or checking behaviors help reduce your anxiety or make you feel better, even if it's just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a moment, yes… or I think it’s more that doing the list or checking the date makes me feel like I’ve done the sensible thing. Yes, everyday things, mostly. It sort of wanders, though. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether I’ve got the dates straight, and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then the gutters on the house—whether they need fixing before it rains again.

It’s more… I don’t know, more just wanting to feel like I’ve kept on top of things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them or think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually the main thing that brought me in. It’s not usually one single thought like a phrase repeating, more like the worrying gets going and I can’t seem to switch it off.\n\nI’ll have made the list, checked the dates, done what I can—my daughter teases me about my little scraps of paper everywhere, though I think she forgets she does the same thing on her phone—and even then my mind keeps circling back. I’ll be trying to read or make dinner, and it’s still there, nagging at me, even when I tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that come into your mind unwanted and uninvited?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they are unwanted in the sense that I’m not inviting them in. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Right, let’s worry for the next hour.” It just starts up.\n\nI’ll have done the sensible things — made my list, checked the dates, made sure I haven’t forgotten something — and then I’ll be washing a cup or looking for my glasses, which are usually on my head, silly as that is, and the worry is right back there again. I tell myself I’ve done all I reasonably can, but it doesn’t seem to switch off just because I say so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think certain thoughts or have certain mental images to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… no. I mean, I do think things through over and over, but not in the sense that I have to picture something or say something in my head to keep everyone safe, if that’s what you mean.\n\nMy sister used to say a little prayer before long drives—well, not my sister, actually, my aunt did that—and I suppose lots of people have little habits like that. But for me, it’s more that my mind wanders back to the worry. I haven’t noticed having a particular thought or image that I feel I must have, or else something bad will happen.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, everyday things, mostly. It sort of wanders, though. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether I’ve got the dates straight, and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then the gutters on the house—whether they need fixing before it rains again.\n\nThey’re not dramatic things on their own, I know that. But in my head they start to feel as if one small thing could unravel into a much bigger problem. Then I’m off down that road before I’ve even noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done what I can,” but my mind just keeps circling back anyway.\n\nIt’s silly, because I’ve always been a list person—my husband used to tease me about having lists for my lists—but lately it doesn’t give me the relief it used to. I’ll have everything written down and still sit there thinking, “Did I miss something?” and then I’m off again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over, like checking things multiple times?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, I’ll look back at my calendar or a note more than once sometimes, but I’ve always been the sort of person who likes things written down properly. My mother was like that too—she had this little address book she kept for years, with everyone’s birthdays in it, and she’d rewrite it when it got messy.\n\nBut, um… as far as repeating actions over and over, I’m not sure I’d call it that. It’s more that I sometimes want to be certain I haven’t forgotten something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying or checking behaviors help reduce your anxiety or make you feel better, even if it's just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a moment, yes… or I think it’s more that doing the list or checking the date makes me feel like I’ve done the sensible thing. My father used to say, “Measure twice, cut once,” though he was talking about shelves, not appointments.\n\nBut the trouble is, even after I’ve made the list and checked what I needed to check, I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it starts. I’ll tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can,” and still my mind keeps going back to it. So it doesn’t really make me feel better for long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors in a precise way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a ritual, exactly. I mean, I do like things done a certain way sometimes, but I think that’s just how I’ve always been. My kitchen cupboards, for instance—well, actually my daughter says I put the mugs in the “wrong” place, so perhaps it’s not even that organized.\n\nBut no, I don’t have a particular thing I feel I must do just so, or something bad will happen if I don’t. At least, I haven’t noticed anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, more just wanting to feel like I’ve kept on top of things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them or think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually the main thing that brought me in. It’s not usually one single thought like a phrase repeating, more like the worrying gets going and I can’t seem to switch it off.\n\nI’ll have made the list, checked the dates, done what I can—my daughter teases me about my little scraps of paper everywhere, though I think she forgets she does the same thing on her phone—and even then my mind keeps circling back. I’ll be trying to read or make dinner, and it’s still there, nagging at me, even when I tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that come into your mind unwanted and uninvited?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they are unwanted in the sense that I’m not inviting them in. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Right, let’s worry for the next hour.” It just starts up.\n\nI’ll have done the sensible things — made my list, checked the dates, made sure I haven’t forgotten something — and then I’ll be washing a cup or looking for my glasses, which are usually on my head, silly as that is, and the worry is right back there again. I tell myself I’ve done all I reasonably can, but it doesn’t seem to switch off just because I say so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think certain thoughts or have certain mental images to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… no. I mean, I do think things through over and over, but not in the sense that I have to picture something or say something in my head to keep everyone safe, if that’s what you mean.\n\nMy sister used to say a little prayer before long drives—well, not my sister, actually, my aunt did that—and I suppose lots of people have little habits like that. But for me, it’s more that my mind wanders back to the worry. I haven’t noticed having a particular thought or image that I feel I must have, or else something bad will happen.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, everyday things, mostly. It sort of wanders, though. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether I’ve got the dates straight, and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then the gutters on the house—whether they need fixing before it rains again.\n\nThey’re not dramatic things on their own, I know that. But in my head they start to feel as if one small thing could unravel into a much bigger problem. Then I’m off down that road before I’ve even noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think probably it is, though I don’t always see it at the time. It feels reasonable while I’m in it, you know?\n\nMy mind keeps roaming from one thing to the next — the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then suddenly I’m thinking about whether the gutters need fixing before the rain comes. And the annoying thing is, each one is a real enough thing, not imaginary exactly. But they get bigger in my head. A small loose end starts to feel like it could unravel into something much worse.\n\nI suppose that does sound out of proportion when I say it out loud.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "Determine if the patient's worrying is causing significant distress or impairment in their daily life", "question": "Has your worrying been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?"}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, yes. Not in a dramatic way—I mean, I still get out in the garden, and I still go to book club. I haven’t stopped everything.

But I do notice I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. For book club I’ll sometimes skim instead of really reading, which isn’t like me, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started saying no to bits I probably would have taken on before. There was a time I liked being useful with all that—lists, dates, who’s bringing what, that sort of thing. My mother was terribly organized, actually, she had a drawer for every kind of receipt—

But yes, sorry. The point is, I think I’m pulling back from some of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to.

I’ll be trying to read or make dinner, and it’s still there, nagging at me, even when I tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that come into your mind unwanted and uninvited?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they are unwanted in the sense that I’m not inviting them in. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Right, let’s worry for the next hour.” It just starts up.\n\nI’ll have done the sensible things — made my list, checked the dates, made sure I haven’t forgotten something — and then I’ll be washing a cup or looking for my glasses, which are usually on my head, silly as that is, and the worry is right back there again. I tell myself I’ve done all I reasonably can, but it doesn’t seem to switch off just because I say so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think certain thoughts or have certain mental images to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… no. I mean, I do think things through over and over, but not in the sense that I have to picture something or say something in my head to keep everyone safe, if that’s what you mean.\n\nMy sister used to say a little prayer before long drives—well, not my sister, actually, my aunt did that—and I suppose lots of people have little habits like that. But for me, it’s more that my mind wanders back to the worry. I haven’t noticed having a particular thought or image that I feel I must have, or else something bad will happen.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, everyday things, mostly. It sort of wanders, though. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether I’ve got the dates straight, and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then the gutters on the house—whether they need fixing before it rains again.\n\nThey’re not dramatic things on their own, I know that. But in my head they start to feel as if one small thing could unravel into a much bigger problem. Then I’m off down that road before I’ve even noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think probably it is, though I don’t always see it at the time. It feels reasonable while I’m in it, you know?\n\nMy mind keeps roaming from one thing to the next — the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then suddenly I’m thinking about whether the gutters need fixing before the rain comes. And the annoying thing is, each one is a real enough thing, not imaginary exactly. But they get bigger in my head. A small loose end starts to feel like it could unravel into something much worse.\n\nI suppose that does sound out of proportion when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. Not in a dramatic way—I mean, I still get out in the garden, and I still go to book club. I haven’t stopped everything.\n\nBut I do notice I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. For book club I’ll sometimes skim instead of really reading, which isn’t like me, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started saying no to bits I probably would have taken on before. There was a time I liked being useful with all that—lists, dates, who’s bringing what, that sort of thing. My mother was terribly organized, actually, she had a drawer for every kind of receipt—\n\nBut yes, sorry. The point is, I think I’m pulling back from some of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

It’s distressing, yes… not unbearable, exactly, but it gets under my skin. My mind starts roaming from one thing to another—the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then whether the gutters need fixing—and before I know it, each little thing feels bigger than it should.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done what I can,” but my mind just keeps circling back anyway.\n\nIt’s silly, because I’ve always been a list person—my husband used to tease me about having lists for my lists—but lately it doesn’t give me the relief it used to. I’ll have everything written down and still sit there thinking, “Did I miss something?” and then I’m off again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over, like checking things multiple times?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, I’ll look back at my calendar or a note more than once sometimes, but I’ve always been the sort of person who likes things written down properly. My mother was like that too—she had this little address book she kept for years, with everyone’s birthdays in it, and she’d rewrite it when it got messy.\n\nBut, um… as far as repeating actions over and over, I’m not sure I’d call it that. It’s more that I sometimes want to be certain I haven’t forgotten something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying or checking behaviors help reduce your anxiety or make you feel better, even if it's just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a moment, yes… or I think it’s more that doing the list or checking the date makes me feel like I’ve done the sensible thing. But for me, it’s more that my mind wanders back to the worry. I haven’t noticed having a particular thought or image that I feel I must have, or else something bad will happen.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, everyday things, mostly. It sort of wanders, though. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether I’ve got the dates straight, and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then the gutters on the house—whether they need fixing before it rains again.\n\nThey’re not dramatic things on their own, I know that. But in my head they start to feel as if one small thing could unravel into a much bigger problem. Then I’m off down that road before I’ve even noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think probably it is, though I don’t always see it at the time. It feels reasonable while I’m in it, you know?\n\nMy mind keeps roaming from one thing to the next — the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then suddenly I’m thinking about whether the gutters need fixing before the rain comes. And the annoying thing is, each one is a real enough thing, not imaginary exactly. But they get bigger in my head. A small loose end starts to feel like it could unravel into something much worse.\n\nI suppose that does sound out of proportion when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. Not in a dramatic way—I mean, I still get out in the garden, and I still go to book club. I haven’t stopped everything.\n\nBut I do notice I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. For book club I’ll sometimes skim instead of really reading, which isn’t like me, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started saying no to bits I probably would have taken on before. There was a time I liked being useful with all that—lists, dates, who’s bringing what, that sort of thing. My mother was terribly organized, actually, she had a drawer for every kind of receipt—\n\nBut yes, sorry. The point is, I think I’m pulling back from some of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're worrying about these everyday things?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing, yes… not unbearable, exactly, but it gets under my skin. My mind starts roaming from one thing to another—the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then whether the gutters need fixing—and before I know it, each little thing feels bigger than it should.\n\nIt’s that feeling that if I don’t keep thinking it through, something small might unravel into a much bigger problem. And then I’m tense and preoccupied, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done what I can,” but my mind just keeps circling back anyway.\n\nIt’s silly, because I’ve always been a list person—my husband used to tease me about having lists for my lists—but lately it doesn’t give me the relief it used to. I’ll have everything written down and still sit there thinking, “Did I miss something?” and then I’m off again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over, like checking things multiple times?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, I’ll look back at my calendar or a note more than once sometimes, but I’ve always been the sort of person who likes things written down properly. My mother was like that too—she had this little address book she kept for years, with everyone’s birthdays in it, and she’d rewrite it when it got messy.\n\nBut, um… as far as repeating actions over and over, I’m not sure I’d call it that. It’s more that I sometimes want to be certain I haven’t forgotten something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying or checking behaviors help reduce your anxiety or make you feel better, even if it's just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a moment, yes… or I think it’s more that doing the list or checking the date makes me feel like I’ve done the sensible thing. My father used to say, “Measure twice, cut once,” though he was talking about shelves, not appointments.\n\nBut the trouble is, even after I’ve made the list and checked what I needed to check, I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it starts. I’ll tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can,” and still my mind keeps going back to it. So it doesn’t really make me feel better for long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors in a precise way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a ritual, exactly. I mean, I do like things done a certain way sometimes, but I think that’s just how I’ve always been. My kitchen cupboards, for instance—well, actually my daughter says I put the mugs in the “wrong” place, so perhaps it’s not even that organized.\n\nBut no, I don’t have a particular thing I feel I must do just so, or something bad will happen if I don’t. At least, I haven’t noticed anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, more just wanting to feel like I’ve kept on top of things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them or think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually the main thing that brought me in. It’s not usually one single thought like a phrase repeating, more like the worrying gets going and I can’t seem to switch it off.\n\nI’ll have made the list, checked the dates, done what I can—my daughter teases me about my little scraps of paper everywhere, though I think she forgets she does the same thing on her phone—and even then my mind keeps circling back. I’ll be trying to read or make dinner, and it’s still there, nagging at me, even when I tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that come into your mind unwanted and uninvited?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they are unwanted in the sense that I’m not inviting them in. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Right, let’s worry for the next hour.” It just starts up.\n\nI’ll have done the sensible things — made my list, checked the dates, made sure I haven’t forgotten something — and then I’ll be washing a cup or looking for my glasses, which are usually on my head, silly as that is, and the worry is right back there again. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether I’ve got the dates straight, and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then the gutters on the house—whether they need fixing before it rains again.\n\nThey’re not dramatic things on their own, I know that. But in my head they start to feel as if one small thing could unravel into a much bigger problem. Then I’m off down that road before I’ve even noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think probably it is, though I don’t always see it at the time. It feels reasonable while I’m in it, you know?\n\nMy mind keeps roaming from one thing to the next — the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then suddenly I’m thinking about whether the gutters need fixing before the rain comes. And the annoying thing is, each one is a real enough thing, not imaginary exactly. But they get bigger in my head. A small loose end starts to feel like it could unravel into something much worse.\n\nI suppose that does sound out of proportion when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. Not in a dramatic way—I mean, I still get out in the garden, and I still go to book club. I haven’t stopped everything.\n\nBut I do notice I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. For book club I’ll sometimes skim instead of really reading, which isn’t like me, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started saying no to bits I probably would have taken on before. There was a time I liked being useful with all that—lists, dates, who’s bringing what, that sort of thing. My mother was terribly organized, actually, she had a drawer for every kind of receipt—\n\nBut yes, sorry. The point is, I think I’m pulling back from some of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're worrying about these everyday things?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing, yes… not unbearable, exactly, but it gets under my skin. My mind starts roaming from one thing to another—the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then whether the gutters need fixing—and before I know it, each little thing feels bigger than it should.\n\nIt’s that feeling that if I don’t keep thinking it through, something small might unravel into a much bigger problem. And then I’m tense and preoccupied, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform any specific physical actions, like handwashing or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like washing my hands over and over, or counting things. I mean, I do sometimes get up and check something practical if it’s bothering me—like looking at the calendar or making a note—but that’s more… trying to settle myself, I suppose.\n\nMy mother used to count stitches when she knitted, and she’d get quite cross if anyone interrupted her—though that’s not really what you asked. But no, I haven’t noticed any particular physical thing I feel driven to do like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I’m not sure I’d call them routines, exactly. I do go over things in my head sometimes, but it’s more like… trying to make sense of them, not because I have a set phrase or pattern I must do.

Though I suppose I can get a bit stuck on a line of thought—like replaying whether I’ve forgotten something—and then I’ll remember something completely different, like how my father used to leave notes tucked in books instead of using a diary. We were always finding little scraps years later.

Sorry, I’m drifting. No, I don’t think I have a particular mental ritual I feel I must do to stop the worrying. At least, I haven’t noticed one.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done what I can,” but my mind just keeps circling back anyway.\n\nIt’s silly, because I’ve always been a list person—my husband used to tease me about having lists for my lists—but lately it doesn’t give me the relief it used to. I’ll have everything written down and still sit there thinking, “Did I miss something?” and then I’m off again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over, like checking things multiple times?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, I’ll look back at my calendar or a note more than once sometimes, but I’ve always been the sort of person who likes things written down properly. My mother was like that too—she had this little address book she kept for years, with everyone’s birthdays in it, and she’d rewrite it when it got messy.\n\nBut, um… as far as repeating actions over and over, I’m not sure I’d call it that. It’s more that I sometimes want to be certain I haven’t forgotten something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying or checking behaviors help reduce your anxiety or make you feel better, even if it's just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a moment, yes… or I think it’s more that doing the list or checking the date makes me feel like I’ve done the sensible thing. My father used to say, “Measure twice, cut once,” though he was talking about shelves, not appointments.\n\nBut the trouble is, even after I’ve made the list and checked what I needed to check, I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it starts. I’ll tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can,” and still my mind keeps going back to it. So it doesn’t really make me feel better for long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors in a precise way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a ritual, exactly. I mean, I do like things done a certain way sometimes, but I think that’s just how I’ve always been. My kitchen cupboards, for instance—well, actually my daughter says I put the mugs in the “wrong” place, so perhaps it’s not even that organized.\n\nBut no, I don’t have a particular thing I feel I must do just so, or something bad will happen if I don’t. At least, I haven’t noticed anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, more just wanting to feel like I’ve kept on top of things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them or think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually the main thing that brought me in. It’s not usually one single thought like a phrase repeating, more like the worrying gets going and I can’t seem to switch it off.\n\nI’ll have made the list, checked the dates, done what I can—my daughter teases me about my little scraps of paper everywhere, though I think she forgets she does the same thing on her phone—and even then my mind keeps circling back. I’ll be trying to read or make dinner, and it’s still there, nagging at me, even when I tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that come into your mind unwanted and uninvited?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they are unwanted in the sense that I’m not inviting them in. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Right, let’s worry for the next hour.” It just starts up.\n\nI’ll have done the sensible things — made my list, checked the dates, made sure I haven’t forgotten something — and then I’ll be washing a cup or looking for my glasses, which are usually on my head, silly as that is, and the worry is right back there again. I tell myself I’ve done all I reasonably can, but it doesn’t seem to switch off just because I say so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think certain thoughts or have certain mental images to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… no. I mean, I do think things through over and over, but not in the sense that I have to picture something or say something in my head to keep everyone safe, if that’s what you mean.\n\nMy sister used to say a little prayer before long drives—well, not my sister, actually, my aunt did that—and I suppose lots of people have little habits like that. I mean, I do think things through over and over, but not in the sense that I have to picture something or say something in my head to keep everyone safe, if that’s what you mean.\n\nMy sister used to say a little prayer before long drives—well, not my sister, actually, my aunt did that—and I suppose lots of people have little habits like that. But for me, it’s more that my mind wanders back to the worry. I haven’t noticed having a particular thought or image that I feel I must have, or else something bad will happen.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, everyday things, mostly. It sort of wanders, though. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether I’ve got the dates straight, and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then the gutters on the house—whether they need fixing before it rains again.\n\nThey’re not dramatic things on their own, I know that. But in my head they start to feel as if one small thing could unravel into a much bigger problem. Then I’m off down that road before I’ve even noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think probably it is, though I don’t always see it at the time. It feels reasonable while I’m in it, you know?\n\nMy mind keeps roaming from one thing to the next — the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then suddenly I’m thinking about whether the gutters need fixing before the rain comes. And the annoying thing is, each one is a real enough thing, not imaginary exactly. But they get bigger in my head. A small loose end starts to feel like it could unravel into something much worse.\n\nI suppose that does sound out of proportion when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. Not in a dramatic way—I mean, I still get out in the garden, and I still go to book club. I haven’t stopped everything.\n\nBut I do notice I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. For book club I’ll sometimes skim instead of really reading, which isn’t like me, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started saying no to bits I probably would have taken on before. There was a time I liked being useful with all that—lists, dates, who’s bringing what, that sort of thing. My mother was terribly organized, actually, she had a drawer for every kind of receipt—\n\nBut yes, sorry. The point is, I think I’m pulling back from some of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're worrying about these everyday things?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing, yes… not unbearable, exactly, but it gets under my skin. My mind starts roaming from one thing to another—the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then whether the gutters need fixing—and before I know it, each little thing feels bigger than it should.\n\nIt’s that feeling that if I don’t keep thinking it through, something small might unravel into a much bigger problem. And then I’m tense and preoccupied, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform any specific physical actions, like handwashing or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like washing my hands over and over, or counting things. I mean, I do sometimes get up and check something practical if it’s bothering me—like looking at the calendar or making a note—but that’s more… trying to settle myself, I suppose.\n\nMy mother used to count stitches when she knitted, and she’d get quite cross if anyone interrupted her—though that’s not really what you asked. But no, I haven’t noticed any particular physical thing I feel driven to do like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think through certain thoughts or mental routines to try and stop your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them routines, exactly. I do go over things in my head sometimes, but it’s more like… trying to make sense of them, not because I have a set phrase or pattern I must do.\n\nThough I suppose I can get a bit stuck on a line of thought—like replaying whether I’ve forgotten something—and then I’ll remember something completely different, like how my father used to leave notes tucked in books instead of using a diary. We were always finding little scraps years later.\n\nSorry, I’m drifting. No, I don’t think I have a particular mental ritual I feel I must do to stop the worrying. At least, I haven’t noticed one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So it doesn’t really make me feel better for long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors in a precise way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a ritual, exactly. I mean, I do like things done a certain way sometimes, but I think that’s just how I’ve always been. My kitchen cupboards, for instance—well, actually my daughter says I put the mugs in the “wrong” place, so perhaps it’s not even that organized.\n\nBut no, I don’t have a particular thing I feel I must do just so, or something bad will happen if I don’t. At least, I haven’t noticed anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, more just wanting to feel like I’ve kept on top of things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them or think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually the main thing that brought me in. It’s not usually one single thought like a phrase repeating, more like the worrying gets going and I can’t seem to switch it off.\n\nI’ll have made the list, checked the dates, done what I can—my daughter teases me about my little scraps of paper everywhere, though I think she forgets she does the same thing on her phone—and even then my mind keeps circling back. I’ll be trying to read or make dinner, and it’s still there, nagging at me, even when I tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that come into your mind unwanted and uninvited?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they are unwanted in the sense that I’m not inviting them in. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Right, let’s worry for the next hour.” It just starts up.\n\nI’ll have done the sensible things — made my list, checked the dates, made sure I haven’t forgotten something — and then I’ll be washing a cup or looking for my glasses, which are usually on my head, silly as that is, and the worry is right back there again. I tell myself I’ve done all I reasonably can, but it doesn’t seem to switch off just because I say so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think certain thoughts or have certain mental images to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… no. I mean, I do think things through over and over, but not in the sense that I have to picture something or say something in my head to keep everyone safe, if that’s what you mean.\n\nMy sister used to say a little prayer before long drives—well, not my sister, actually, my aunt did that—and I suppose lots of people have little habits like that. But for me, it’s more that my mind wanders back to the worry. I haven’t noticed having a particular thought or image that I feel I must have, or else something bad will happen.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, everyday things, mostly. It sort of wanders, though. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether I’ve got the dates straight, and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then the gutters on the house—whether they need fixing before it rains again.\n\nThey’re not dramatic things on their own, I know that. But in my head they start to feel as if one small thing could unravel into a much bigger problem. Then I’m off down that road before I’ve even noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think probably it is, though I don’t always see it at the time. It feels reasonable while I’m in it, you know?\n\nMy mind keeps roaming from one thing to the next — the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then suddenly I’m thinking about whether the gutters need fixing before the rain comes. And the annoying thing is, each one is a real enough thing, not imaginary exactly. But they get bigger in my head. A small loose end starts to feel like it could unravel into something much worse.\n\nI suppose that does sound out of proportion when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. Not in a dramatic way—I mean, I still get out in the garden, and I still go to book club. I haven’t stopped everything.\n\nBut I do notice I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. For book club I’ll sometimes skim instead of really reading, which isn’t like me, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started saying no to bits I probably would have taken on before. There was a time I liked being useful with all that—lists, dates, who’s bringing what, that sort of thing. My mother was terribly organized, actually, she had a drawer for every kind of receipt—\n\nBut yes, sorry. The point is, I think I’m pulling back from some of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're worrying about these everyday things?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing, yes… not unbearable, exactly, but it gets under my skin. My mind starts roaming from one thing to another—the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then whether the gutters need fixing—and before I know it, each little thing feels bigger than it should.\n\nIt’s that feeling that if I don’t keep thinking it through, something small might unravel into a much bigger problem. And then I’m tense and preoccupied, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform any specific physical actions, like handwashing or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like washing my hands over and over, or counting things. I mean, I do sometimes get up and check something practical if it’s bothering me—like looking at the calendar or making a note—but that’s more… trying to settle myself, I suppose.\n\nMy mother used to count stitches when she knitted, and she’d get quite cross if anyone interrupted her—though that’s not really what you asked. But no, I haven’t noticed any particular physical thing I feel driven to do like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think through certain thoughts or mental routines to try and stop your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them routines, exactly. I do go over things in my head sometimes, but it’s more like… trying to make sense of them, not because I have a set phrase or pattern I must do.\n\nThough I suppose I can get a bit stuck on a line of thought—like replaying whether I’ve forgotten something—and then I’ll remember something completely different, like how my father used to leave notes tucked in books instead of using a diary. We were always finding little scraps years later.\n\nSorry, I’m drifting. No, I don’t think I have a particular mental ritual I feel I must do to stop the worrying. At least, I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform any specific actions, like checking or arranging things, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in an overwhelming way. I might straighten a pile of papers if I’m already looking at them, or check the calendar once if something is on my mind, but I don’t feel driven to keep doing it.\n\nArranging things—well, I’ve always liked the kitchen counter clear, but that’s probably from my grandmother, she was very particular about surfaces. She used to say you could tell the state of a person’s mind by their sink, which I never thought was very fair.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed a specific action I feel I must do because of the worrying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "determine presence of compulsive behaviors", "question": "Do you feel like you have to repeat any behaviors over and over again in order to feel okay or to prevent something bad from happening?"}

I’ll have everything written down and still sit there thinking, “Did I miss something?” and then I’m off again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over, like checking things multiple times?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, I’ll look back at my calendar or a note more than once sometimes, but I’ve always been the sort of person who likes things written down properly. My mother was like that too—she had this little address book she kept for years, with everyone’s birthdays in it, and she’d rewrite it when it got messy.\n\nBut, um… as far as repeating actions over and over, I’m not sure I’d call it that. It’s more that I sometimes want to be certain I haven’t forgotten something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying or checking behaviors help reduce your anxiety or make you feel better, even if it's just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a moment, yes… or I think it’s more that doing the list or checking the date makes me feel like I’ve done the sensible thing. My father used to say, “Measure twice, cut once,” though he was talking about shelves, not appointments.\n\nBut the trouble is, even after I’ve made the list and checked what I needed to check, I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it starts. I’ll tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can,” and still my mind keeps going back to it. I mean, I do like things done a certain way sometimes, but I think that’s just how I’ve always been. My kitchen cupboards, for instance—well, actually my daughter says I put the mugs in the “wrong” place, so perhaps it’s not even that organized.\n\nBut no, I don’t have a particular thing I feel I must do just so, or something bad will happen if I don’t. At least, I haven’t noticed anything like that. It’s more… I don’t know, more just wanting to feel like I’ve kept on top of things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them or think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually the main thing that brought me in. It’s not usually one single thought like a phrase repeating, more like the worrying gets going and I can’t seem to switch it off.\n\nI’ll have made the list, checked the dates, done what I can—my daughter teases me about my little scraps of paper everywhere, though I think she forgets she does the same thing on her phone—and even then my mind keeps circling back. I’ll be trying to read or make dinner, and it’s still there, nagging at me, even when I tell myself, “You’ve done all you reasonably can.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that come into your mind unwanted and uninvited?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose they are unwanted in the sense that I’m not inviting them in. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Right, let’s worry for the next hour.” It just starts up.\n\nI’ll have done the sensible things — made my list, checked the dates, made sure I haven’t forgotten something — and then I’ll be washing a cup or looking for my glasses, which are usually on my head, silly as that is, and the worry is right back there again. I tell myself I’ve done all I reasonably can, but it doesn’t seem to switch off just because I say so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think certain thoughts or have certain mental images to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… no. I mean, I do think things through over and over, but not in the sense that I have to picture something or say something in my head to keep everyone safe, if that’s what you mean.\n\nMy sister used to say a little prayer before long drives—well, not my sister, actually, my aunt did that—and I suppose lots of people have little habits like that. But for me, it’s more that my mind wanders back to the worry. I haven’t noticed having a particular thought or image that I feel I must have, or else something bad will happen.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, everyday things, mostly. It sort of wanders, though. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether I’ve got the dates straight, and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then the gutters on the house—whether they need fixing before it rains again.\n\nThey’re not dramatic things on their own, I know that. But in my head they start to feel as if one small thing could unravel into a much bigger problem. Then I’m off down that road before I’ve even noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think probably it is, though I don’t always see it at the time. It feels reasonable while I’m in it, you know?\n\nMy mind keeps roaming from one thing to the next — the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then suddenly I’m thinking about whether the gutters need fixing before the rain comes. And the annoying thing is, each one is a real enough thing, not imaginary exactly. But they get bigger in my head. A small loose end starts to feel like it could unravel into something much worse.\n\nI suppose that does sound out of proportion when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. Not in a dramatic way—I mean, I still get out in the garden, and I still go to book club. I haven’t stopped everything.\n\nBut I do notice I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. For book club I’ll sometimes skim instead of really reading, which isn’t like me, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started saying no to bits I probably would have taken on before. There was a time I liked being useful with all that—lists, dates, who’s bringing what, that sort of thing. My mother was terribly organized, actually, she had a drawer for every kind of receipt—\n\nBut yes, sorry. The point is, I think I’m pulling back from some of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're worrying about these everyday things?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing, yes… not unbearable, exactly, but it gets under my skin. My mind starts roaming from one thing to another—the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then whether the gutters need fixing—and before I know it, each little thing feels bigger than it should.\n\nIt’s that feeling that if I don’t keep thinking it through, something small might unravel into a much bigger problem. And then I’m tense and preoccupied, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform any specific physical actions, like handwashing or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. But no, I haven’t noticed any particular physical thing I feel driven to do like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think through certain thoughts or mental routines to try and stop your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them routines, exactly. I do go over things in my head sometimes, but it’s more like… trying to make sense of them, not because I have a set phrase or pattern I must do.\n\nThough I suppose I can get a bit stuck on a line of thought—like replaying whether I’ve forgotten something—and then I’ll remember something completely different, like how my father used to leave notes tucked in books instead of using a diary. We were always finding little scraps years later.\n\nSorry, I’m drifting. No, I don’t think I have a particular mental ritual I feel I must do to stop the worrying. At least, I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform any specific actions, like checking or arranging things, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in an overwhelming way. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether I’ve got the dates straight, and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then the gutters on the house—whether they need fixing before it rains again.\n\nThey’re not dramatic things on their own, I know that. But in my head they start to feel as if one small thing could unravel into a much bigger problem. Then I’m off down that road before I’ve even noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think probably it is, though I don’t always see it at the time. It feels reasonable while I’m in it, you know?\n\nMy mind keeps roaming from one thing to the next — the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then suddenly I’m thinking about whether the gutters need fixing before the rain comes. And the annoying thing is, each one is a real enough thing, not imaginary exactly. But they get bigger in my head. A small loose end starts to feel like it could unravel into something much worse.\n\nI suppose that does sound out of proportion when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. Not in a dramatic way—I mean, I still get out in the garden, and I still go to book club. I haven’t stopped everything.\n\nBut I do notice I’m not quite as prepared as I used to be. For book club I’ll sometimes skim instead of really reading, which isn’t like me, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started saying no to bits I probably would have taken on before. There was a time I liked being useful with all that—lists, dates, who’s bringing what, that sort of thing. My mother was terribly organized, actually, she had a drawer for every kind of receipt—\n\nBut yes, sorry. The point is, I think I’m pulling back from some of it because it feels harder to manage than it used to.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're worrying about these everyday things?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing, yes… not unbearable, exactly, but it gets under my skin. My mind starts roaming from one thing to another—the exhibition schedule, then my daughter’s travel plans, then whether the gutters need fixing—and before I know it, each little thing feels bigger than it should.\n\nIt’s that feeling that if I don’t keep thinking it through, something small might unravel into a much bigger problem. And then I’m tense and preoccupied, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform any specific physical actions, like handwashing or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like washing my hands over and over, or counting things. I mean, I do sometimes get up and check something practical if it’s bothering me—like looking at the calendar or making a note—but that’s more… trying to settle myself, I suppose.\n\nMy mother used to count stitches when she knitted, and she’d get quite cross if anyone interrupted her—though that’s not really what you asked. But no, I haven’t noticed any particular physical thing I feel driven to do like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think through certain thoughts or mental routines to try and stop your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them routines, exactly. I do go over things in my head sometimes, but it’s more like… trying to make sense of them, not because I have a set phrase or pattern I must do.\n\nThough I suppose I can get a bit stuck on a line of thought—like replaying whether I’ve forgotten something—and then I’ll remember something completely different, like how my father used to leave notes tucked in books instead of using a diary. We were always finding little scraps years later.\n\nSorry, I’m drifting. No, I don’t think I have a particular mental ritual I feel I must do to stop the worrying. At least, I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform any specific actions, like checking or arranging things, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in an overwhelming way. I might straighten a pile of papers if I’m already looking at them, or check the calendar once if something is on my mind, but I don’t feel driven to keep doing it.\n\nArranging things—well, I’ve always liked the kitchen counter clear, but that’s probably from my grandmother, she was very particular about surfaces. She used to say you could tell the state of a person’s mind by their sink, which I never thought was very fair.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed a specific action I feel I must do because of the worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat any behaviors over and over again in order to feel okay or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I do. I mean, there are things I prefer a certain way, but I don’t repeat them over and over to stop something happening.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back at a note I’ve written, but that’s just because my handwriting has got worse over the years—my old schoolteacher would have had something to say about it. She used to make us rewrite whole pages if the margins weren’t neat.\n\nBut no… not behaviors I feel I have to keep repeating until I feel okay. At least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not so much that I’ve stopped seeing people. I still go to book club, and I keep up with the garden—my roses are actually doing better than I expected, though that may just be the rain and not anything I’ve done.\n\nBut I do notice I’m less prepared when I show up to things. And with the exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down some of the bits I would’ve taken on before, because it just feels harder to manage than it used to. I don’t think anyone’s angry with me, exactly, but I feel a bit bad about not pulling my weight the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I can still enjoy things, yes… I haven’t stopped going to book club, and I’m still out in the garden. The garden’s probably the one place I don’t feel quite so—well, except when the slugs get to the lettuces, but that’s a different sort of problem.

But I have noticed I’m not as prepared as I used to be when I go to things. I’ll arrive having forgotten to read something properly, or not having brought what I meant to bring. And with the exhibition planning, I’ve started saying no to parts I’d normally take on, because it just feels harder to keep everything straight than it used to. So I’m still doing things, but maybe not quite with the same ease.
